Everyone’s busy now, aren’t they? My grandson’s got some job with computers, I can’t make heads or tails of it. But no, I haven’t really noticed not wanting to see people or anything like that. I suppose I just like things a bit quieter than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling more down or blue than usual lately, even if just a little? I still do my little routines. I just don’t make as much fuss over things as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way for most of the day, more days than not, for at least the last two weeks?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? I don’t know… I don’t really keep track of it like that. Well… I wouldn’t say food doesn’t interest me exactly. It’s just that I don’t feel I need as much as I used to. My wife, she’s the one who thinks I’m not eating enough—that’s mostly why I’m here, I suppose.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that things taste worse, exactly. I still like the idea of certain foods, I suppose, but I just don’t end up eating much of them.

What really brought me in is my wife saying I’m not eating enough. Most days I’ll just have coffee and toast in the morning, and maybe some soup later, or vegetables if they’re around. Big meals feel unnecessary at my age, you know. My mother used to make these huge Sunday dinners—roast, potatoes, the whole thing—and I remember everyone sitting around the table for hours. But now it’s just the two of us, and it feels like a lot of fuss for not much reason.

I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I have quiet days, certainly, but at my age a lot of days are quiet anyway. I still putter around, read the paper, sit in the garden if the weather’s decent.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose so. I mean, I don’t feel ill, not in the way you’d think would put someone off food. It’s more that my wife thinks I’m not eating enough.

Most days I just have coffee and toast in the morning, and then maybe some soup later on, or vegetables if there are some made. A big meal just feels… unnecessary at my age. My father used to insist on meat and potatoes every night, rain or shine, but I can’t see the point of all that now. It just seems like more than I need.

I don’t know… I mean, I still do the things I do. I read the paper, I go out to the garden, watch a bit of television in the evening. Whether I enjoy them as much as I used to—well, that’s hard to say, isn’t it?

When you get older, things change. I used to spend whole Saturdays fixing things around the house, and I had a neighbor who’d come over and we’d talk for half the afternoon about nothing at all. He moved away years ago. So it’s not that I’ve lost interest exactly… it’s just quieter now, I suppose.

I mean, I don’t feel ill, not in the way you’d think would put someone off food. A big meal just feels… unnecessary at my age. My father used to insist on meat and potatoes every night, rain or shine, but I can’t see the point of all that now. It just seems like more than I need.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things as you used to, even in activities you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still do the things I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I still go out to the garden when the weather’s good, though half the time I end up just looking at what needs doing rather than doing it. But that might be because the knees aren’t what they used to be—well, you know how that goes.

So no, I can’t really say I don’t care about those things. It’s just different than it used to be.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife mostly, she’s the one who said I ought to come in. She thinks I’m not eating enough. I don’t really see it quite that way. I mean, at my age, you don’t need these great big plates of food like when you’re younger. I usually just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and later on maybe some soup, or vegetables if there are any made up. \n\nMy wife’s always been one for proper meals, you know, meat and potatoes and all that. Her mother was the same way, never let anyone leave the table hungry. But I just don’t feel big meals are necessary anymore. So… that’s mostly why I’m here, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently? And have you been feeling down or losing interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor did say the number on the scale has gotten too low. He made a bit of a fuss about it last time, wrote it down and all. But I don’t know… being lighter isn’t the worst thing. It makes it easier for me to get around the garden, and I still like taking my walks when the weather’s decent.\n\nAs for feeling down, I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. I just don’t think I need as much food as everyone seems to think I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still putter around with things. The garden, for instance—though this time of year there’s always more weeds than anything useful, and the rabbits get into it if you don’t watch them.\n\nFamily, well, they come by when they can. Everyone’s busy now, aren’t they? My grandson’s got some job with computers, I can’t make heads or tails of it. But no, I haven’t really noticed not wanting to see people or anything like that. I suppose I just like things a bit quieter than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling more down or blue than usual lately, even if just a little? Not necessarily sad, but maybe less interested or less motivated?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, no. I mean, I have slower days, same as anyone my age. Some mornings it takes me a while to get moving, especially if it’s damp out—my old neighbor used to say you could feel rain in your bones before the radio knew about it.\n\nBut feeling blue… I don’t really think so. I still do my little routines. I just don’t make as much fuss over things as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way for most of the day, more days than not, for at least the last two weeks?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? I don’t know… I don’t really keep track of it like that. Some days I’m just slower, or I sit with my tea longer than I meant to, but then I’ll notice the birds at the feeder or remember I need to check on something outside.\n\nTwo weeks, you said? I couldn’t say for sure. Time gets a bit slippery when you’re retired—Mondays and Thursdays don’t feel as different as they used to. I haven’t noticed anything like being that way all day, every day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost weight without trying to, or that your clothes are getting looser?\n\nPatient: Well… my doctor did say the number on the scale’s gotten too low. I don’t know, I don’t fuss much with the scale myself. Clothes, maybe some things hang a little different, but half my shirts are old enough they’ve stretched every which way anyway.\n\nI suppose I feel lighter, and that doesn’t seem all bad to me. It makes it easier getting around the garden, bending here and there, and keeping up with my walks. Though the path by my place has these roots coming through now—you have to watch your step or you’ll catch a toe.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to eat, even when you’re hungry, or has food just not interested you much lately?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say food doesn’t interest me exactly. It’s just that I don’t feel I need as much as I used to. My wife, she’s the one who thinks I’m not eating enough—that’s mostly why I’m here, I suppose.\n\nMost mornings I’ll have coffee and a piece of toast, and then maybe later some soup, or vegetables if there are some ready. Big meals just feel… unnecessary at my age. We used to have proper dinners every night when the children were around, but now it seems like an awful lot of fuss for just the two of us.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that food doesn’t appeal to you as much anymore, even when you know you should eat or when you’re hungry?\n\nPatient: I suppose… well, my wife would say I’m not eating enough, and that’s probably why she pushed me to come in. I don’t know that food has no appeal, exactly. It’s more that I just don’t feel I need a whole lot.\n\nMost mornings I have my coffee and a bit of toast, and then later maybe some soup, or vegetables if we’ve got them. Big meals just feel unnecessary at my age. When the children were still at home we’d do proper dinners, but now it seems like an awful lot of bother for just the two of us.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t really feel like eating, even when it’s time for a meal or when you know you should eat something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose… though I wouldn’t put it quite like I’m refusing it. It’s more that when mealtime comes around, I just don’t feel I need much. My wife says I’m not eating enough, and that’s what’s got her worried.\n\nMost days it’s coffee and a bit of toast in the morning, then maybe some soup later, or vegetables if we’ve got them. A big meal just feels unnecessary at my age. We used to sit down to fuller dinners when the kids were home, but now, well… it seems like a lot of fuss.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that food doesn’t taste as good anymore, or that you’ve lost interest in eating even the foods you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that things taste worse, exactly. I still like the idea of certain foods, I suppose, but I just don’t end up eating much of them.\n\nWhat really brought me in is my wife saying I’m not eating enough. Most days I’ll just have coffee and toast in the morning, and maybe some soup later, or vegetables if they’re around. Big meals feel unnecessary at my age, you know. My mother used to make these huge Sunday dinners—roast, potatoes, the whole thing—and I remember everyone sitting around the table for hours. But now it’s just the two of us, and it feels like a lot of fuss for not much reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down, discouraged, or lost interest in most activities for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I don’t know if I’d say it’s because they don’t satisfy me. That sounds a bit too neat, maybe.

I mean, at my age, you don’t need these great big plates of food like when you’re younger. I usually just have coffee and a piece of toast in the morning, and later on maybe some soup, or vegetables if there are any made up. \n\nMy wife’s always been one for proper meals, you know, meat and potatoes and all that. Her mother was the same way, never let anyone leave the table hungry. But I just don’t feel big meals are necessary anymore. And have you been feeling down or losing interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor did say the number on the scale has gotten too low. He made a bit of a fuss about it last time, wrote it down and all. But I don’t know… being lighter isn’t the worst thing. It makes it easier for me to get around the garden, and I still like taking my walks when the weather’s decent.\n\nAs for feeling down, I wouldn’t say that’s the main thing. I just don’t think I need as much food as everyone seems to think I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with family?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I’ve lost interest, exactly. I still putter around with things. The garden, for instance—though this time of year there’s always more weeds than anything useful, and the rabbits get into it if you don’t watch them.\n\nFamily, well, they come by when they can. Everyone’s busy now, aren’t they? My grandson’s got some job with computers, I can’t make heads or tails of it. But no, I haven’t really noticed not wanting to see people or anything like that. I suppose I just like things a bit quieter than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve been feeling more down or blue than usual lately, even if just a little? I just don’t make as much fuss over things as I used to, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way for most of the day, more days than not, for at least the last two weeks?\n\nPatient: Most of the day? It makes it easier getting around the garden, bending here and there, and keeping up with my walks. Though the path by my place has these roots coming through now—you have to watch your step or you’ll catch a toe.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself not wanting to eat, even when you’re hungry, or has food just not interested you much lately?\n\nPatient: Well… I wouldn’t say food doesn’t interest me exactly. I don’t know that food has no appeal, exactly. Big meals just feel unnecessary at my age. When the children were still at home we’d do proper dinners, but now it seems like an awful lot of bother for just the two of us.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t really feel like eating, even when it’s time for a meal or when you know you should eat something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose… though I wouldn’t put it quite like I’m refusing it. It’s more that when mealtime comes around, I just don’t feel I need much. My wife says I’m not eating enough, and that’s what’s got her worried.\n\nMost days it’s coffee and a bit of toast in the morning, then maybe some soup later, or vegetables if we’ve got them. A big meal just feels unnecessary at my age. We used to sit down to fuller dinners when the kids were home, but now, well… it seems like a lot of fuss.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that food doesn’t taste as good anymore, or that you’ve lost interest in eating even the foods you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that things taste worse, exactly. I still like the idea of certain foods, I suppose, but I just don’t end up eating much of them.\n\nWhat really brought me in is my wife saying I’m not eating enough. Most days I’ll just have coffee and toast in the morning, and maybe some soup later, or vegetables if they’re around. Big meals feel unnecessary at my age, you know. My mother used to make these huge Sunday dinners—roast, potatoes, the whole thing—and I remember everyone sitting around the table for hours. But now it’s just the two of us, and it feels like a lot of fuss for not much reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down, discouraged, or lost interest in most activities for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks in a row?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I have quiet days, certainly, but at my age a lot of days are quiet anyway. I still putter around, read the paper, sit in the garden if the weather’s decent.\n\nMy wife says I get too set in my ways, but she’s said that for years. We used to go out more when the children were younger—school events, visits, all that—but life changes, doesn’t it? So… no, I don’t think I’ve been down most of the day every day like that.\n\nDoctor: Has your lack of appetite or reduced interest in eating been happening even when you don’t have any physical discomfort or illness that might explain it?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. I mean, I don’t feel ill, not in the way you’d think would put someone off food. A big meal just feels… unnecessary at my age. My father used to insist on meat and potatoes every night, rain or shine, but I can’t see the point of all that now. It just seems like more than I need.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t take as much pleasure or interest in things as you used to, even in activities you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I still do the things I do. I read the paper, I go out to the garden, watch a bit of television in the evening. Whether I enjoy them as much as I used to—well, that’s hard to say, isn’t it?\n\nWhen you get older, things change. I used to spend whole Saturdays fixing things around the house, and I had a neighbor who’d come over and we’d talk for half the afternoon about nothing at all. He moved away years ago. So it’s not that I’ve lost interest exactly… it’s just quieter now, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few months, have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to look forward to, like gardening, reading, or seeing family, even when you’re not physically tired or unwell?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I still go out to the garden when the weather’s good, though half the time I end up just looking at what needs doing rather than doing it. But that might be because the knees aren’t what they used to be—well, you know how that goes.\n\nReading, I still read the paper. Sometimes I don’t get through all of it, but to be honest there’s only so much news a person wants to hear these days. As for family, I like seeing them when they come by. It’s just… everyone’s busy now, and I don’t like making a fuss.\n\nSo no, I can’t really say I don’t care about those things. It’s just different than it used to be.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say you’ve stopped doing things you used to enjoy, not because of how you feel physically or because of changes in your life, but just because they don’t seem to bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s because they don’t satisfy me. That sounds a bit too neat, maybe.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some things, yes, but… well, life changes, doesn’t it? I used to go down to the hardware shop just to look around, even if I didn’t need anything. There was a fellow there who knew everything about old tools—could talk your ear off about a plane or a hinge. They closed that shop years ago, put in one of those chain places where nobody knows where anything is.\n\nSo I suppose I don’t do certain things as much. But whether that’s because they don’t bring me satisfaction anymore, or because things aren’t the same… I couldn’t really say. I haven’t thought of it that way."
